It's descending steadily. her massive wings flexing slightly under her own weight. The engine spooling down, but then just before touchdown, the aircraft seems to float. The floating sensation isn't just your imagination. It's the aircraft experiencing ground effect. What exactly is ground effect? Now in simple terms, ground effect is a phenomenon that occurs when an aircraft is flying at an altitude roughly equal to or less than its wingspan above the ground. So for the Boeing 747 with a wingspan of about 64 m, that means that effect kicks in when it's flying within 200 ft above the surface. So to understand what's happening here, we need to talk about lift and drag. So when a plane is in cruise flight, air flows over and under the wings, creating pressure difference, lower pressure above the wing and high pressure below the wing. Now this difference generates lift. But here's the catch. Some of that high pressure air from beneath the wing tries to sneak around the wing to the low pressure area on the top, creating swirling vortices. Now these vortices cause something called induced drag and reduce overall lift. Now this vortex is the beginning of the famous wake turbulence. Now enter the ground. When the aircraft gets close to the ground within one wingspan, the surface interferes with these vortices. It blocks or traps the swirling air from forming fully. And as a result, the aircraft suddenly experiences less drag and a noticeable increase in lift. That's why the aircraft seems to float or cushion above the runway. Compare this to trying to push a beach ball underwater. Naturally, it wants to come back up again. Now, that added lift makes the plane more buoyant, so to speak, but it can also catch inexperienced pilots offguard. Now, let's talk about the pros of ground effect. First off, the increased lift and reduced drag can make takeoffs more efficient. Airlines operating heavy aircraft like the 747 or the AC50 etc. can benefit from slightly shorter takeoff rolls especially at sea level airports. Now the aircraft gets airborne a bit sooner saving wear on the landing gear and reducing the runway distance required. Now this effect can be particular helpful when flying from wet or contaminated runways where any extra bit of lift can make a difference. The reduced drag also improves fuel efficiency during these initial moments after liftoff. Now, in all fairness, it isn't much, but every little helps. Now, here comes the flip side. Now, because ground effect makes the aircraft fly more efficiently, it can create a false sense of performance. One of the biggest cons is premature liftoff. Especially in GA airplanes, a pilot might rotate at the correct angle of attack and feel the plane becoming airborne, but without actually reaching a safe climb speed. The aircraft may be flying, yes, but it's stuck in sort of an aerodynamic limbo, unable to climb out of the ground effect. Meaning on takeoff, this can negatively impact performance if the pilot rotates too early and lifts off at too low an air speed. So the apparent lift is then increased in ground effect. So the aircraft may seem ready to fly, but once it climbs out of the ground effect, that extra lift disappears, induced drag increases and the aircraft might struggle to maintain flight. And this can result in setting back onto the runway or worse, a stall shortly after takeoff if there's not enough air speed or power. So in short, taking off too early in ground effect can give a full sense of sufficient lift, leading to a dangerous loss of performance when the aircraft climbs out of the ground effect. The second major con appears on landing. as you approach the runway with a bit too much speed. Grand effect can cause the aircraft to float just like our 747 at the beginning. Now that floating makes it difficult to judge the right moment to flare and touch down and you might end up using significantly more runway than expected. This effect is exaggerated in long body aircraft like the Airbus A340 or the lady here in the background. Its massive wings generate an enormous ground cushion which means it can float longer than smaller aircraft. Now at busy airports where every meter counts, this can be a real operational challenge. So let's break it down. The pros of ground effect are increased lift close to the ground, reduced induced drag, shorter takeoff distances, improved initial fuel efficiency slightly. Let's put that way. The cons of the ground effect are premature liftoff without sufficient climb performance, risk of floating during landing, especially at higher speeds, misleading cues for inexperienced pilots, and potential runway overruns. ## 19. How an AFTERBURNER works - Explained by CAPTAIN JOE
**Channel:** Captain Joe | **Views:** 98K | **Date:** 11 months ago | **Duration:** 9:43 | **ID:** g3awv7eeubA
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=g3awv7eeubA

### Transcript:
dear friends and followers welcome back to my channel and to another jet engine related video within this small series about jet engines imagine soaring or powering at supersonic speeds blazing through the skies with a raw power that leaves a trail of fire behind this is the SR71 Blackbird in action an iconic example of an aircraft that had afterburners fitted to its jet engines these afterburners are what allowed this aircraft and many others to reach extraordinary speeds unlocking their full potential but what exactly is an After Burner and how does it work to deliver such a significant boost in thrust Buck love and find out in a minute by just looking at the word After Burner we can already tell that something is being being burned after something so basically speaking an After Burner is an additional combustion chamber added to a jet engine it's mainly used in military aircraft that need an extra boost for its task like supersonic flight rapid takeoffs and intense combat Maneuvers in an After Burner fuel is injected directly into the hot exhaust gases after the engines turbine allowing the afterburner to create a second more powerful combustion process this in turn significantly increases thrust without requiring a bigger heavier engine we'll use the General Electric j79 turbo jet engine famously powering the f104 locked star fighter the science behind the afterburner let's break down the science of how afterburners work now jet engines including afterburners rely on Newton's third law for every action there's an equal and opposite reaction this law is the heart of jet propulsion where thrust is generated by accelerating air backwards pushing the aircraft forward now in my previous videos you saw me using the thrust formula which in its simplest form describes thrust as being proportional to the mass of air flowing through the engine per unit of time multiplied by the difference in speed of the air that's flowing into the engines and the air that is being expelled by the exhaust nozzle in a conventional jet engine air is first compressed then mixed with fuel ignited and expelled at high speeds through a nozzle to create thrust but with an After Burner fuel is injected into the already hot exhaust gases after they've passed through the turbine creating an additional combustion process that reignites the exhaust gases all right a quick dive into into the physics behind it for all of you who like the more technical insights remember the thermodynamic process which I explained the basics of it in the first video about the turbo jet engine if not I highly recommend you to watch it now what the afterburner does it raises the temperature of the air flow by burning additional fuel in the exhaust stream after the energy necessary to power the engine itself has already been extracted by the turbine similar to the combustion chamber this process is ideally done at constant pressure raising the enthalpy of the fluid as seen in this diagram this drastically increases the kinetic energy of the air flow resulting in an additional increase in thrust compared to an engine without an After Burner how is an afterburner designed for all of this to work afterburners have to have additional fuel spray bars or combustion rings fitted behind a turbine to inject fuel into the exhaust furthermore most afterburners feature structures called The Flame holders which help stabilize the combustion in terms of temperature control the inner casing of the After Burner needs to be able to withstand the temperatures created by the additional combustion which can be an excess of 1,000° C therefore After Burner sections need special Coatings or even cooling ribs and can usually not be operated for a prolonged time integrating an After Burner influences every aspect of an engine's design for instance turbo Jets which are optimized for Speed pair well with afterburners but they are relatively fuel inefficient even before adding an afterburner turbo fans on the other hand balance efficiency and thrust by combining a core jet stream with cooler bypassed air when afterburners are added to Turbo fans which really do exist this bypass air helps the afterburner achieve better fuel efficiency and enhanced thrust output this makes after burning turbo fans also called augmented turbo fans ideal for modern fighter jets like the Euro Fighter for example in some Advanced engines thrust can be further optimized by burning fuel in a specific section of the exhaust stream for example the praton Whitney tf30 featured separate zones for its bypass and core flows allowing for a more controlled and efficient after burning process other engines like the Rolls-Royce spay mix both flows before after burning to produce an intense easily be distinguished from turbine blades they are much thinner and lighter given the fact that the structural and thermal stresses acting upon them are far much smaller than those acting upon in the turbine as we can tell from the braen cycle we need to increase the energy and de pressure of the air flow so in the compressor this is mainly achieved by forcing the air through the converging path and as you can see the path the air needs to go through becomes narrower and narrower throughout the compressor to understand the concepts behind compressors and turbines it is important to know that only the rotating components are doing the work and either put energy into the airf flow or extract it as for example the turbine blades would do in case of the compressor stage the first set of blad blades are rotor blades they are responsible for increasing the flows energy namely pressure and temperature they are also introducing an angular momentum or a Twist to the flow which then needs to be taken out of the flow again because ideally the rotor blades need a Twist free flow to work this is where the state blades come into play because of their orientation and their profile the twist Twisted flow is deflected and approaches the next stage Twisted free every stage is responsible for a specific increase in pressure leading to the flow reaching its maximum pressure before entering the combustion chamber the ratio between the air pressure in front of the compressor and the one at the end right here just before entering the combustion chamber is one of two major design parameters and is called the op PR the overall pressure ratio as an example this engine right here has an opr of 3.42 to 1 it is limited by the structural Integrity of the compressor one big challenge When developing compressors is the increasing pressure gradient throughout the compressor since the ambient air pressure in front of the compressor is far below the pressure at the end the air constantly tries to flow up stream in order to equalize the overall pressure in the system now the compressor needs to work against that discontinuously and flawlessly in order to overcome this gradient and increase the pressure this is the reason why there are more compressor stages than there are turbine stages the flow in the turbine is meant to flow through the turbine towards a low pressure area the flow in the compressor is not its pressure is meant to be increasing which works best by increasing it stagewise and overcoming multiple smaller gradients one stage at a time this by the way is an advantage of the radial jet engine which is able to overcome bigger gradients with less stages however one reason why axal jet engines are used nowadays is that the possible mass flow of it is by far greater than that of a radial engine fun fact if if for some reason the flow through the compressor is Disturbed a reverse flow can be the result which then would lead to a compressor stall short Interruption would you want us to record a video on radial jet engines please comment below combustion chamber coming out of the compressor the air will enter the combustion chamber where jet fuel is introduced through the fuel nozzles right here modern engines have so-called air fuel spray nozzles installed which optimize the atomization of the mixture and therefore the burning behavior of the flame igniter plugs are also used to ignite the mixture during startup or situations where continuous ignition is required to prevent a flame out under ideal conditions fuel is continuously burned with no further need of to ignite a plugs until the engine is shut off still the plugs can be activated manually or automatically if the combustion is Disturbed or the engine needs to be restarted the purpose of the combustion chamber is to increase the energy of the Flow by converting the chemical energy of the fuel into thermal energy when we think of the brighten cycle again it is the key component that allows for an increase in inner energy while moving up on the isobar at the same time at the end of the combustion chamber the temperature reaches its maximum this temperature is the second of the two major design parameters and is so-called T the turbine entry temperature it is strictly limited by the materials used for the turbine blades the problem is that those temperatures can easily be couple of hundred degrees above the actual melting point of the blade only through special cooling techniques it is possible to operate at those high temperature without the turbine blades melting turbine work speaking of turbine blades let's take a look at them other than the stages in a compressor the stages of an axile turbine have a flipped rotor stat Arrangement so the first are doing the work need to be approached by a Twist induced air flow in order to draw the energy from the flow other than in the compressor the stator Blades of a turbine introduce a Twist to the flow lightning now from the air it can look like a pulsing glow or flashes of light inside a thunderstorm and pilots flying near thunderstorms often see these Eerie light shows it's quite stunning to look at while it's not as dangerous as a direct lightning strike it's a good reminder that the storm is brewing and it's time to give it space and avoid them hail the silent danger While most of us think of hail as golf ball size ice chunks falling from the sky up a cruising altitude hailstones can be even bigger and more damaging Pilots avoid hail storms at all costs because even a short encounter can cause DSE in the airplanes fuselage crack the windshield or damage the engines the scariest part hailstones can sometimes travel sideways out of a storm so even planes steering clear of the main thund the cloud can get caught off guard and pl that have gone through hail clouds are sometimes damaged Beyond repair I have not encountered that myself but I've seen planes land after me that are completely I'm not going to say trashed but yes uh Beyond repairable the green flash a blink and you'll miss it this next phenomenon is a super rare treat for Pilots the green flash it happens during sunrise or Sunset when the sun is just below the Horizon and the atmosphere acts like a prism bending the light for a split second a green light flashes above the sun's Edge now seeing the Green Flash is incredibly rare but pilots flying above the clouds and water sometimes get the perfect view it's not a weather Hazard just one of those magical moments that remind you how amazing our atmosphere is ozone layer by providing a surface for chemical reactions that break down the ozone molecules so they're both stunning and sobering at the same time microburst the heavens tsunami a microburst is a small but powerful downdraft of air that occurs during thunderstorms and spreads out rapidly upon hitting the ground now these intense downdrafts can cause sudden and severe changes in wind speed and direction creating a wind shear Hazard microbursts typically last only a few minutes but can produce wind speeds of over a 100 knots posing a significant danger to aircraft for Pilots encountering a microburst during takeoff or Landing is especially dangerous as the sudden loss of lift and drastic wind changes can lead to a crash affected aircraft may experience an initial headwind followed by a sudden Tailwind making recovery difficult so microbursts are challenging to predict but can often be detected with Doppler radar or onboard wind shear detection systems clear air turbulence the invisible speed bump finally let's talk about something every passenger has felt but never seen clear air turbulence this phenomenon occurs in Clear Skies with no visible clouds it's caused by changes in wind speed or Direction in the atmosphere often near jet streams now the weird thing about clear air turbulence is that it can happen out of nowhere making it hard to predict Pilots rely solely on reports from other pilots and weather forecasts to avoid them but sometimes it is unavoidable it's a good reminder to always keep your seat belt fastened even if the skies look calm but passengers one of the most persistent myths is that toilets can suck you in if you flush while sitting down dear friends and followers welcome back to my Channel today we're talking about something every air traveler has experienced but probably never thought about the humble airplane bathroom Believe It or Not these tiny often awkward spaces have a fascinating history from buckets to highend vacuums the evolution of airplane bathrooms tells a story of innovation necessity and some times a little bit of chaos so let's dive in pun absolutely intended and here we [Music] go the early days no bathrooms at all let's start at the very beginning the first airplanes didn't even have bathrooms early flights like the R Brothers in 1903 were too short and too basic to even consider such a luxury passengers in the 1910s and 1920s if there were any were expected to hold it until they landed in fact early commercial flights were so noisy and bumpy that bathrooms were probably the last thing on anyone's mind so these trips were short hops lasting maybe an hour or two and people often wore goggles and heavy coats because the cabins weren't even pressurized and sometimes even partially open so that the passengers were EXP exposed to the elements Comfort wasn't a priority survival was the first inflight toilets by the 1930s air travel started becoming more mainstream and with longer flights came the need for onboard restrooms enter the bucket and curtain system Yes you heard that right early airplanes like the Handley page hp42 had a designated area with a bucket for waste and a curtain for privacy it wasn't ideal but it was a start these setups were far from hygienic and emptying the bucket after the flight was a task no crew member wanted to do can you imagine modern Travelers putting up with that we've come a long way World War II The Flying latrines during World War II the need for bathrooms on planes took a big Leap Forward military bombers like the B17 Flying Fortress had long missions sometimes lasting over 10 hours pilots and crew couldn't just land to take a break so Engineers came up with a basic toilet systems called relief tubes now these were essentially funnels connected to the hoses that discharged waste outside the plane it sounds efficient right well not exactly at high altitudes the tube sometimes froze creating let's call it a back pressure it wasn't glamorous but it worked most of of the time the first Laboratories in commercial flights the 1940s and the 1950s marked the arrival of more sophisticated airplane bathrooms for commercial flights planes like the Douglas DC3 started including small fully enclosed Laboratories now these weren't much larger than the closet and often came with chemical toilets which used blue liquid to break down the waste even though these bathrooms were a huge Improvement they were still very basic passengers might have access to a sink but running water was rare and the waste was often stored on board until the plane landed still it was a step forward or towards the modern bathroom experience we know today the jet age bathrooms get an upgrade with the dawn of the jet age in the 1960s airplanes got bigger faster and more luxurious and so did their bathrooms aircrafts like the conic Boeing 707 and a Douglas dc8 featured more advanced Laboratories with flushing systems and running water these planes also introduced a concept of waste tanks which stored everything in a sealed compartment to be emptied after landing now gone were the days of dumping waste mid-flight thankfully for the people on the ground another big change was the introduction of multiple bathrooms on larger planes for the first time passengers didn't have to wait forever in line at least in theory luxury airliners even added powder rooms with mirrors and extra space especially in first class the Advent of vacuum toilets the real game changer came in the 1980s with the introduction of the vacuum toilet this is the system most modern planes use today instead of relying on water or chemicals vacuum toilets use suction to whisk waste away into holding tanks why is this such a big deal for one it's far more efficient and vacuum toilets use less water which reduce the overall weight of the plane less weight means less fuel consumption which is good for Airlines and the environment plus the sealed tanks significantly reduce odors a win for everyone on board quirks and mishaps bathroom myths and legends of course airplane bathrooms haven't been without their quirks and controversies one of the most persistent myths is that toilets can suck you in if you flush while sitting down let's clear this up it is not true the vacuum system is powerful but not that powerful there's also the famous case of the blue ice speed number one is the one that I would deem the easiest to understand it is the indicated air speed to have a better understanding of it let's have a look how basic air speed measurement is done air speed is being measured by obtaining a differential pressure so basically speaking an air speeed indicator is a barometric instrument the air around you has a certain pressure in the standard atmosphere for example this pressure equals one 13 hectopascals at sea level now this pressure is called Static air pressure and you can remember that by picturing that it will always act upon you even if you're standing still in other words when you are static now when an object starts moving through the air there will be a second pressure acting upon on it and this pressure is the dynamic air pressure you have all experienced Dynamic air pressure before by just holding your hand out of the window of a driving car at some point in your childhood maybe now this Dynamic pressure can be mathematically expressed by half of the air density times the speed squared now both of these pressures the static and the dynamic pressure will result in the total pressure that a moving object is experiencing now this equation is called Boli's equation and is the foundation for basic air speed measurements now to do this our aircraft needs to be equipped with a so-called ptotic system this usually consists of a Pito tube which is a cylindrical tube that has an opening facing the oncoming airflow and a static Port which is an opening preferably on the side of the aircraft's fuselage and perpendicular to the oncoming air flow now this system allows for a measurement of the total air pressure through the PTO tube and the static air pressure through the static Port now looking at bol's equation again we can see that we can indeed derive the speed just from these two measurements as mentioned before indicated air speed is therefore a differential pressure expressed in terms of speed knots most commonly or kilomet per hour but what about the density in this equation well since density variations affect the pressure probes in the same way they affect the aircraft's performance namely lift the air speed indicators are also reliable with density fluctuations now just picture fewer air molecules that both hit the wings and the probes within less dense air so technically the aircraft has to fly faster through the air to ruce the lift required but since fewer air particles will hit the probes too the indicated air speed will not necessarily change compared to the scenario with higher air density what definitely changes though is the true air speed and the ground speed but that is something we'll discuss later on now that we have a establish the first basic air speed and what's behind it we can now dive deeper which is necessary because there are a lot of error sources that affect the indicated air speed and make it less accurate the first errors to address are the so-called instrument and position errors now since air that is moving over objects of irregular shape like an aircraft fuselage is subject to pressure fluctuations making the measured pressure values by the probes distorted now with increasing speed these distortions need to be accounted for which is normally done by applying Corrections found in the aircraft flight manual now for smaller general aviation aircraft like single engine piston aircraft these Corrections are usually neglected because of their lower speed but once you apply the corrections to the indicated air speed you will get these so-called calibrated AirSpeed short cast [Music] now that we have our calibrated air speed it's time to add another layer to our understanding the equivalent air speed or EAS as we fly faster compressibility effects start playing a role unlike water for example air is a compressible fluid meaning that it will react with a pressure increase when being compressed into smaller volume now the same happens with the air in the front of the PTO tube which is compressed by the motion of the aircraft through the air mass the so-called Ram effect causes slight inaccuracies in how the pressure difference is interpreted by our instruments which is particularly noticeable at high altitudes and high speeds now the ram effect will lead to a higher total pressure being sensed which if not corrected will lead to a higher speed indication ES compensates for this effect by doing a compressibility correction giving us a more true sense of our speed in relation to the actual aerodynamic forces acting on the aircraft in modern aircraft this is usually done electronically by the air data computer now onto the true air speed or Tas which is probably one of the more crucial if not the most crucial air speed while es accounts for compressibility TAS accounts for density variations giving us the aircraft's actual speed relative to the surrounding air mass when Pilots file a flight pan or communicate with air traffic control Tas is often the speed they refer to as it accurately represents how fast they're actually traveling through the air itself at sea level Tas and AAS are typically very close but as the aircraft climbs task gradually increases in relation to the IAS due to the thinner air for example if we're cruising at a high altitude with an IAS of 250 knots your Tas might be closer to 400 knots as I said at the beginning of the video your indicated air speed gauge can only show what is being measured now with increasing altitude there is less air density so fewer air molecules per volume of air this simply means that fewer a molecules will enter the probe which has the same effect as if you were flying slower at low altitude does that make sense now this indication however is not the real speed through the air the real speed is your Tas so Tas can be calculated by applying a density correction to the esas which for slow speeds and low altitudes can be approximated to 2% % increase per 1,000 ft of altitude gained I'm sure that if you are a flight student you have heard this in flight school before why is this important you might ask now Tas can be used for many things it is essential for estimating how long it will take to reach a destination when there is no wind and it's the basis for many aspects of flight planning particular for fuel consumption calculations and distance estimations furthermore Tas is used for many performance figures and calculations for example for the bank angle necessary to fly a turn at a standard rate or the turn radius at a given Bank Angle now procedure designers use these figures to design arrival and departure procedures for airports however please note that the speeds they write into the charts for example the limit speeds for certain turns Etc are indeed indicated air speed simply because in aviation we fly according to the indicated air speed and not the true air speed ground speed or GS is the speed that most closely resembles what people think of the actual speed in daily life as it represents how fast the aircraft is moving relative to the ground ground speed is simply the task adjusted for wind conditions if you're flying with a Tailwind your ground speed will be faster faster than your task while when you have a headwind it obviously will be reduced the easiest way to explain ground speed is do you see the shadow of our airplane moving over the ground yes the speed of your Shadow is your ground speed but to use an example if our Tas is 400 knots and you're cruising with a 50 knot Tailwind your ground speed would be 450 knots on the other hand a 50 knot headwind would drop your ground speed to 350 knots ground speed is the speed that determines your estimate time of arrival at your destination and is crucial for navigation and fuel management especially on L haul flights where winds can significantly impact flight time further the ground speed can be used to calculate the point of equal times meaning the point on your route at which it would take you as much time to continue to destination as it would take you to turn back to the departure Airdrome or alternate air ground speed can also be used to calculate the point of safe return which is the last point on the route which allows for the time to turn around and return to the departure Aerodrome within the safe endurance margin meaning without having to use fuel that's reserved for contingencies or emergencies both these points are heavily influenced by winds and form an integral part of the flight planning process similar to the task ground speed is also also used for performance calculations one quick example would be the calculation of the required syn rate on a 3° approach slope like most approaches have now to obtain an estimate of this rate Pilots learn to multiply their ground speed times five meaning that if your approach speed is I don't know 100 knots a 500 ft per minute de centrate on your vertical speed indicator should keep you on profile this works because a head or Tailwind significantly shortens or lengthens to ground distance at a given syn rate that's why Pilots have to correct the syn rate according to their ground speed finally we arrive at the Mac number which is especially important for high speeds and high altitude flight typically in Jets now the ma number is the ratio of the aircraft's true air speed to the local speed of sound important to note since the speed of sound decreases as the temperature decreases but therefore the altitude increases Mac number becomes a vital reference for ensuring the aircraft remains within safe operating limits as it speeds up for instance a Mac number of one means you're flying at the speed of sound while Mac 85 means you're flying at 8 5% of the speed of sound now this is quite the eye opener for many I guess the Mac number is critical when flying close to the transonic range that's around Mac 75 to Mac 1.2 where aerodynamic forces around the aircraft can change dramatically and create shock waves now commercial airliners often cruise around Mac 78 or 85 and the higher they fly the ner the margins of attainable speeds they become this is because as discussed so often already in this video the air density decreases with altitude therefore the aircraft has to fly faster to achieve the necessary lift meaning that the lower boundary of permissible air speeds at altitude is limited by the stall speed or in this case called the aerodynamic limit on Modern pfds this limit is displayed by a red bar coming from the bottom of the speed tape the upper limit however is given by the air speed at which the aircraft will go into overs speed at high altitudes the limiting factors are transonic and supersonic effects such as a shock wave leading to flow separation buffeting or heavy vibrations of the airframe and in extreme cases structural failures those transonic speeds need to be be monitored by the use of the Mac number and on Modern pfds the equivalent limit speed will be marked by the end of yet another red bar coming from the top of the speed tape at a certain altitude depending on the aircraft's weight for example these two bars will meet when they meet the aircraft has reached the so-called coffin corner why is it called coffin corner well think of it if you were to fly slower you would immediately stall and if you are to fly faster you will encounter flow separation and stall to due to the shock effects and risk breaking the aircraft this is especially dangerous in turbulent flight conditions as fluctuations in speed can easily push the aircraft beyond the coffin corner and Into the Danger area that is why Pilots closely monitor their speed when they near the coffin corner and keep an altitude and speed margin to safely stay away from it to summarize let's quickly revisit each speed we started with the IAS the indicated air speed the raw speed read from the instruments which were corrected for position and instrument errors to get Cass Cass the calibrated air speed is Ias adjusted for positioning errors giving a more accurate reading at lower speeds and altitudes then we had the e as the equivalent air speed that is the cast adjusted for compressibility effects especially relevant at higher speeds and high altitudes then we have the Tas the true air speed the aircraft's actual speed through the air mass essentially for navigation and flight planning and then we had GSD ground speed which is Tas adjusted for wind determining the true speed over the ground and finally the Mac number the the ratio of Tas to the speed of sound and it is essential for highspeed flight and avoiding shock wve effects understanding these air speeds is not just a technical requirement but a practical necessity for safe and efficient flight allowing Pilots to gauge performance plan accurately and ensure safety at any altitude or speed last but not least to help you learn and remember all these speeds I encourage you to have a look at the IC e model it looks like the square root symbol and quickly shows the magnitude of these speeds during normal flight now the Cass is only slightly larger than the I due to the positioning and instrument errors then the e is a Fab bit lower than both of them due to the ram effect producing incorrect High readings so the es has to be corrected downwards the magnitude of the correction depends on the aircraft's speed though and finally the Tas which depending on altitude will normally be much higher than all of them and in addition to that the ground speed is simply the task corrected up or downwards depending on the wing conditions and the Mac number is a speed figure on its own but is nothing more than the ratio of the task to the local speed of sound yes that was a lot but that sums up the air speed Bible if you have any more questions or explanatory notes regarding air speeds feel free to use the comment section below and if you have some other Aviation related questions please be sure to check out my other videos or ask in the comment section for the chance to have your questions answered in a future video and on that bombshell thank you very much for your time here's your checklist today subscribe to my channel check activate the notification Bell check follow my Instagram account check storms can pull the ADF need toward them instead of the NDB leading to incorrect readings so you are basically getting headed towards the thunderstorm dear friends and followers welcome back to my to find your way in today's video we're going to explore this classic navigation tool that can enhance your flying skills and help you navigate safely when technology may fail so get your notebooks out because there's a small test at the end of this video so no messing about and let's get [Music] started what do NDB and ADF sand for first things first the NDB is a nondirectional beacon it is a very simple yet essential form of navigation especially in light aircraft it can also be used in Airline jets for long distance or approach navigation it consists of a transmitter based on the ground that transmits radio waves in every direction hence the name non-directional Beacon this works in conjunction with the ADF the automatic direction finder which is an instrument fitted in your plane after you have dialed in the NDB frequen quency the ADF searches for the radio waves and points its Needle on the instrument towards the tuned in NDB now speaking of the frequencies this little symbol here shows the location of the ndbs on your VFR IFR charts plus their frequency a two or three letter identifier and the mors code to explain it in the simplest way possible possible picture your friend saying to you do you see that light on the top of the mountain right there you reply yeah I can see that then you point with your finger to that light very simply speaking the light is the NDB and the tip of your finger is the ADF needle pointing towards it the light or let's call it a beacon shines in all directions so nondirectional and you can see it from anywhere where you're standing but The Shining Light can canot tell your position relative to it to avoid any confusion and ensure you are flying towards the correct Beacon each NDB transmits its identification in the low or medium frequency bands somewhere between 200 and 400 kohtz being the most common but they can go all the way up to 1,750 khz now each NDB will transmit it its own two or three-letter mulse code signal for example the Plymouth NDB has a frequency of 397 khz and is identified by listening to the Papa yane Morse code signal sounds confusing don't worry the frequency source and identification information are on the radio navigation charts or the approach charts that you will be carrying in the cockpit what is the range of a nondirectional beacon well if you are flying long range you will need a fairly strong NDB now these will have a range of 100 nautical miles or more however for even longer crosswater flying you will need an even more powerful indb for example some beacons in the Pacific area can have a range of up to 400 nautical miles on the other hand if you're flying a much shorter route within the UK for example where there are many navigation facilities most of the ndbs have a much shorter range going back to our Plymouth NDB which just has a 20 nautical mile radius basically acting as a homing Beacon as it is situated right next to the runways of Plymouth airport there are a few effects on the NDB that will affect its accuracy that you should be aware of under ideal conditions an NDB signal will be accurate to to within 2° but several factors can reduce this accuracy considerably one of the most common one is interferences from other ndbs that transmit on similar frequencies so you must ensure that your ADF is tuned perfectly the Earth itself also has an effect NDB signals can reflect of high mountains which kind of sort of distort the signal and so can Thunder all I hate this word Kum limbus loud formations causing electrical storms can pull the ADF knel toward them instead of the NDB leading to incorrect reading so you are basically getting headed towards the thunderstorm now that we have found our Direction with the NDB how does our aircraft tell us where to go the automatic direction finder operates on the radio Compass principle meaning that it will point the directional needle toward where the signals from the NDB are coming from there are three components within the aircraft that make up an ADF the receiver this is where the frequency is tuned to match the NDB the aerial system this is made up of a loop aial and a sense AAL which work together to identify exactly where the NDB signals are coming from on all the airplane the antenna is often referred to the airplane's washing line as it was attached at the vertical stabiliz izer all the way to the roof of the cockpit and the ADF display obviously located among the cockpit instruments now in order to identify the correct NDB that you want to fly to you will need to select the audio on the ADF and then listen to the morse code signal to confirm that it is the correct one ndbs have different identification characteristics associated with their trans type for example in the UK all ndbs can be identified using the ADF mode selector only however in Continental Europe some ndbs require the pilot to First select the bfo or the beat frequency oscillator to enable identification of the NDB now the role of the bfo is to impose a tone on the NDB carrier wave to make it audible to the pilot now some ndbs can carry voice Transmissions like the automatic terminal information service atis at some airports in rare cases if there is a malfunction with ATC Communications via the normal VHF signal it is possible for them to send Transmissions via an NDB or VR I have actually experienced that a couple of times already so when flying to or from an NDB understanding bearings is essential now the bearing to an NDB is called the qdm magnetic bearing to the station while the bearing from the NDB is the QDR the magnetic bearing from the station I always used the nemonic when flying to an NDB the m in the qdm is for home and when flying away from it I'm on the QDR and the R means meaning I'm radiating away from the NDB so to fly to the NDB you align your aircraft's heading so that the ADF needle points directly ahead of the relative bearing indicator and another helpful donkey bridge is how to correct your course in a crosswind use the neonics push and pull the needle when tracking to the NDB push the aircraft's heading toward towards the needle if it drifts to the side conversely when flying from the ND pull the tail of the needle towards the desired track to keep the aircraft on course this approach ensures you maintain an accurate bearing relative to the station either When approaching or departing the NDB even with wind interference and speaking of wind you don't want to be homing to the NDB if there's a strong crosswind the aircraft tends to drift sideways resulting in a curved flight path towards the NDB rather than a straight track this happens because the aircraft's heading changes to maintain a direct bearing to the station continuously correcting for the lateral drift caused by the wind to avoid this curving path and instead maintain a straight CA to the NDB you should track towards the NDB Now tracking involves obviously correcting for Wind Drift by setting a wind correction angle so you determine this by flying The aircraft's Heading slightly into the wind such that the needle stays offset by a small amount by doing so this will then allow the aircraft to fly a straight path as you maintain a consistent bearing to the NDB compensating for the crosswind obviously Now by monitoring and adjusting your heading as necessary you maintain a direct straight line track to the NDB this is a super fun practice on and the PC simulations you definitely have to try that out if the audio setting on the ADF is selected you may also be able to receive broadcasting stations such as the BBC or other commercial radio stations since they transmit using low frequency and mid-frequency bands now you might be surprised to hear that some Pilots have been known to using these as navigational AIDS however that is not the best idea as the source can be difficult and unreliable to identify because radio station broadcasts don't always come from the main transmission tower they usually use relay stations to broadcast their signal over wider distances so today we have covered the basics of what a non-directional beacon and an automatic direction finder are and what they look like and how they are used and along with the precautions to bear in mind now here's your test write in the comments of more drag and weight so let's talk about some practical implications for commercial pilots the Y damper can significantly enhance the flying experience for both the pilots and passengers when flying through turbulent air the Y damper Smooths out the ride reducing the feeling of bumps and yolt now passengers often appreciate obviously a smoother flight as it makes the journey more comfortable Additionally the Y damper contributes to fuel efficiency by maintaining steady flight paths the aircraft requires less thrust to overcome drag which can translate to fuel savings over long distances now let's wrap up our discussion on the Y damper this sophisticated system is a prime example of how technology enhances safety and efficiency in aviation by automatically correcting unwanted Y and minimizing d roll effects the Y damper allows pilots to focus on broader operational tasks such as navigation and communication that's it for today thanks very much for watching if you techn called selective availability which degraded the signal so civilian devices would be less precise making it only accurate to 100 m but in 2000 selective availability was turned off and civilian GPS devices instantly became much more accurate getting within 10 m or less of the actual position now that's already pretty good to navigate your car through traffic but for Aviation we need even better accuracy and that's where sbass and gbass come into play let's start with sbass or space based augmentation system esbas improves GPS accuracy by using additional satellites and ground stations to broadcast correction data think of it like having a friend double-checking your calculations while you're working on a math problem the most common esbar in the US called was W or wide area augmentation system and the one in Europe is called egnos the European geostationary navigation overlay service now it uses geostationary satellites which stay at the same spot over the equator to send correction signals to GPS receivers across a large area now just to be clear it moves with the Earth's rotation now this correction data Improv improves GPS accuracy down to 1 M or less making it precise enough for airplanes to use during approaches and landings making sbass essential for safety especially when you're flying in poor visibility straight up it is as accurate as an ILS Cat 1 approach but it is still considered as a non-precision approach gbass or groundbased augmentation system works a bit differently now instead of ring on additional satellites gbass uses ground stations located at airports think of it like a satellite on ground instead of in space which looks like this now these ground stations receive GPS signals just like any other GPS device but because their exact location is already known they can compare the GPS position to their true position and calculate the error in the signal they then broadcast this corrected data to planes approaching the airport gbas is used in GLS approaches which are highly precise Landing systems now this fact allowed the gbass approach to be CL classified as a Precision approach cat one in future it is expected to be available for cat 2 and three as well this makes it the system of the future and should slowly start replacing in classic ILS systems now the benefit of gbass is that it can provide even more accurate Landing guidance and can support multiple approaches at once where the ILS is limited to one approach path per Runway now fun fact the little airport of Breman in the north part of Germany was one of the first airports that installed the groundbased augmentation system or the satellite on ground so how does all of this come together in aviation we use GPS in every phase of flight from planning
